Rio chuckled, her fingers brushing lightly against Agatha’s cheek before she handed her the warm cup of coffee. “Or maybe you just got lucky,” she whispered, a mischievous smile playing on her lips.

Agatha took the cup, her fingers brushing against Rio’s as she did, the brief contact sending a familiar thrill through her. “Maybe,” she said, her voice soft, laced with affection as she lifted the cup to her lips. The warmth seeped into her, grounding her in this rare, quiet moment between them.

But even as they shared this brief reprieve, Agatha knew that nothing between them was ever simple. Their connection was a constant dance between light and shadow, control and surrender. And in that delicate balance, in the teasing banter and shared intimacy, they found something that neither of them could easily name but both were reluctant to let go.

“So,” Agatha said, her voice cutting through the comfortable quiet as she glanced at the plate of croissants, the scent of buttery sweetness filling the air between them. “How long do we have before we meet with Beley?”

Rio’s expression shifted slightly, a flicker of something unreadable passing behind her eyes before she glanced out the window. The sky was still cloaked in darkness, but the faintest slivers of sunlight were beginning to creep through, pushing back the night with their soft, golden hue. It was that in-between time—neither fully night nor day—where shadows still clung to the edges of the world, and everything felt suspended in that fragile moment of transition.

“About half an hour,” Rio repeated, her voice carrying a weight that hadn’t been there moments before. She watched the sunlight fight its way through the dark, each beam a quiet rebellion against the night. The gravity of their impending meeting with Beley pressed at the edges of her thoughts, but what lingered more heavily was Agatha—last night’s insecurities, the flicker of doubt she’d seen in those sharp blue eyes. As much as Rio had savored the jealousy—the fire it had sparked in Agatha—it had left a bitter edge. Seeing Agatha doubt herself, doubt them, was a wound far deeper than any she’d anticipated.

Agatha’s gaze narrowed, catching the subtle shift in Rio’s tone, the tension that hummed beneath her words like a note left unresolved. She didn’t press, though. Not yet. Instead, she allowed the silence to stretch between them, the quiet settling like a protective cloak, shielding them for just a moment longer from the inevitable storm that waited beyond their fragile bubble.

“Half an hour, huh?” Agatha echoed, her voice deceptively light, though Rio could feel the undercurrent of curiosity beneath it, the questions waiting, unspoken. Agatha reached for a croissant, tearing off a piece, her fingers idly toying with the flaky edges as she cast a sidelong glance at Rio.

There was something so achingly familiar in that gesture—how Agatha used the smallest of distractions to steady herself, to push back the heaviness that always seemed to hover just out of reach. Rio’s heart clenched, but she remained still, watching her lover through half-lidded eyes, hoping that whatever lingering doubts had clawed their way into Agatha’s heart last night had faded with the dawn.

“Plenty of time,” Agatha murmured, her voice softer now, the edge slipping away as she finally met Rio’s gaze. A faint smile tugged at her lips, and in that brief moment, the tension between them seemed to ease—like a knot slowly loosening, though not completely undone. They both understood that the quiet wouldn’t last, that soon enough, the world would demand its due, pulling them from this fragile sanctuary they’d carved out of the night’s aftermath.

Rio let her gaze soften as well, though the glint of mischief never truly left her eyes. It was how she kept herself grounded, the playfulness that danced at the edge of every serious moment, keeping the darkness at bay. “Enough time for breakfast,” she teased, a smile curling at the corners of her lips. “And maybe… a few distractions.”

Agatha let out a quiet laugh, shaking her head as she lifted her piece of croissant to her mouth. “Always the distractions with you,” she muttered, though there was no malice in her words, only the warmth of someone who understood the rhythm of their shared chaos.

“Well, you make distractions so easy,” Rio murmured, her voice low, the words laced with a familiar warmth that wrapped around them like a cloak. She tore a delicate piece of croissant and brought it to Agatha’s lips, her movements unhurried, intimate. Agatha parted her lips, accepting the offering, the buttery flakiness melting on her tongue as Rio’s gaze never wavered from hers.

The moment was quiet, deceptively so, yet charged with the unspoken tension that always seemed to linger between them—an ever-present hum, a rhythm they both had come to understand. As Agatha finished, Rio’s fingers brushed against her lips, wiping away the remnants of crumbs with a slow, deliberate touch. It was a simple gesture, but the tenderness behind it made Agatha’s chest tighten, a strange, fluttering sensation that left her feeling more exposed than any passionate night could.

Their eyes locked, and in the silence that followed, something deep and unnameable passed between them. Rio’s dark eyes softened, though the spark of mischief never fully left—like a flame that refused to be extinguished. She tilted her head slightly, studying Agatha as if trying to memorize every detail, every curve, every subtle shift of expression.

“What?” Agatha asked, her voice a soft murmur, though a faint smile tugged at her lips.

Rio’s lips curled into a smirk, but it was tinged with something softer, something vulnerable that Rio rarely allowed to surface. “Just making sure you know how easy it is to be distracted by you,” she said, her thumb lingering at the corner of Agatha’s mouth, tracing the outline of her lips before finally pulling away.

Agatha rolled her eyes, though the warmth in her chest only grew. “Always the charmer,” she teased, but there was no hiding the way her heart raced under Rio’s gaze.

“Only for you,” Rio replied, her tone lighter now, but beneath it, there was an edge of sincerity that neither of them needed to acknowledge. It was there, woven into the space between them, in every touch, every shared glance.

They didn’t need words to understand it.

Leaving their cocoon was hard. The warmth of their shared space, the easy banter, the quiet moments wrapped in soft laughter—it was a sanctuary they both clung to. Neither was ready to face the world outside, not yet. But reality had a way of intruding, even on the most sacred of mornings, and the weight of the mission pulled them reluctantly from the comfort of their retreat.

Rio cast one last glance at Agatha before they left, her expression softening for a heartbeat—something almost tender, almost fragile, flickering in the depths of her dark eyes. Agatha caught it, a silent acknowledgment that neither of them wanted to say aloud. Stepping out of their sanctuary, into the cold air of the outside world, felt like breaking something delicate, a thread that connected them in the quiet.

But the world called, as it always did.

They gathered themselves quickly, leaving a generous tip for the tavern manager. Agatha offered a parting smile, though it was fleeting, already fading into the focus of the task ahead. Rio, too, had shed the easy playfulness of the morning, slipping back into her familiar mask of cool detachment, her eyes scanning the village as they made their way through the still-dark streets.

The village was quiet, the first light of dawn just beginning to stretch across the horizon, painting the sky in muted tones of blue and violet. It was an eerie calm, a silence that carried the weight of the unknown, of what lay ahead. Agatha walked beside Rio, her senses already sharpening, the warmth of their shared intimacy replaced by the cold edge of anticipation. The mission loomed over them, a shadow that neither could ignore.

At the entrance of the village, they spotted Beley—but she wasn’t alone. Behind her was a mob of men, their faces twisted in anger and fear, their torches flickering in the early morning light. As Agatha and Rio drew closer, they saw that Beley’s hands were bound, the rope digging cruelly into her wrists. Her eyes met theirs, filled with a mixture of defiance and fear.

“Witch!” the mob shouted, their voices rising in a hateful chorus that echoed through the stillness of the village.

Agatha’s breath hitched, the familiar sting of rage bubbling up inside her, hot and dangerous. Her instincts screamed for action, her body coiled like a spring ready to explode forward. Before she could fully process the surge of anger, she made a move, her muscles tensing as she prepared to lunge towards the mob. But Rio’s hand shot out, gripping her arm, strong and steady, keeping her rooted in place.

“Agatha,” Rio’s voice was calm, but there was an unmistakable edge of warning in it. Her grip tightened just enough to keep Agatha from bolting. “Not yet.”

Agatha’s heart pounded, her pulse loud in her ears as she tried to shake off the restraint. “They’re going to kill her,” she hissed, her voice low and trembling with barely controlled fury. The mob’s voices were growing louder, more frenzied, and every second felt like an eternity.

“I know,” Rio’s voice was steady, but her eyes were sharp, calculating, scanning the mob, weighing the situation. “But charging in now won’t save her. It’ll only get us all killed.”

Agatha’s fists clenched, her nails biting into her palms as she swallowed the storm brewing inside her. Every fiber of her being screamed to unleash hell upon the mob, to tear apart the men who dared to lay hands on Beley and spit venomous words at their kind. But Rio’s calm voice, a tether in the chaos, pulled her back. Logic cut through the haze of her rage. They couldn’t afford recklessness—not here, not now. As much as it killed her to admit it, Rio was right.

“Wait here,” Rio ordered, her tone leaving no room for argument. The weight of her command pressed against Agatha, and for a brief moment, she fought the urge to disobey. She wanted to fight. She needed to fight. But when her gaze met Rio’s—dark, sharp, and unwavering—Agatha’s breath stilled. There was something in Rio’s eyes, something ancient and immovable, that told her she wouldn’t win this battle, not against Rio’s will. “You’re a witch,” Rio explained, her voice low but steady, laced with that unmistakable edge of authority that always seemed to pull Agatha back from the brink. “They see you, and it’ll be over before we even start. They’ll burn the whole place to the ground with us in it.”

Agatha’s mouth twitched, a mix of defiance and frustration bubbling to the surface. “So do you,” she shot back, the words slipping past her lips before she could stop them. You’re a witch too, she wanted to scream. Why did she have to stay behind? Why did Rio get to fight, while she was left in the shadows?

But Rio’s expression didn’t soften. Instead, it grew colder, a smirk curling at the corners of her mouth—a smirk that sent a shiver down Agatha’s spine. “I’m not a witch,” Rio said, her voice dropping, deepening with a gravity that made Agatha’s pulse quicken. “Not only a witch.”

And the smile she gave then—the slow, wicked curve of her lips, the dark gleam in her eyes—was a reminder of what Rio truly was. Not just a witch, but something far more dangerous. The smirk on her face was one of Death itself, a predator’s grin, knowing that the shadows bent to her will and the veil between life and death was hers to manipulate.

Agatha could feel it then, the weight of Rio’s power, ancient and terrible, wrapping itself around them like a shroud. It wasn’t magic like hers, wild and unpredictable. It was something deeper, darker, a force that had lived through centuries, that had walked the line between life and the void with ease.

Her heartbeat echoed in her ears as she nodded, the fire inside her simmering down, though not extinguished. She wasn’t sure what Rio had in mind, but Agatha knew enough to trust her lover’s darkness. There was a plan. There always was.

Agatha watched, her breath catching as Rio moved toward the mob, her silhouette shifting, no longer just a woman, no longer only a witch—Death itself. The air around her seemed to hum, vibrating with an unseen force, as if the very ground trembled beneath her steps. The men who had been so loud and confident moments ago faltered, some instinctively stepping back, their eyes wide with the sudden chill that enveloped them. They didn’t understand what they were feeling, but they knew something had shifted— something ancient and terrible had entered their midst.

But not all of them were wise enough to sense the danger. A few of the more stubborn men held their ground, gripping their weapons tighter as if that could protect them from what stood before them.

Rio’s voice, low and commanding, sliced through the tension like a blade. “Let her go,” she ordered, her tone carrying the weight of inevitability, the cold, certain authority of someone who had seen the end of all things and walked away unscathed.

The words were simple, but the power in them was undeniable, wrapping around the mob like invisible chains, suffocating them with the finality of their meaning.

One man, braver or perhaps more foolish than the rest, took a step forward, shaking his head, his knuckles white around the handle of his pitchfork. “You have no power here, witch,” he spat, though there was a tremor in his voice, betraying the fear he tried so hard to hide.

Rio’s eyes shifted to him, the smirk on her lips never fading, and Agatha saw the flicker of something dark in her gaze—something that made her heart race faster, both with fear and awe.

“I’m no witch,” Rio replied, her voice dropping even lower, like the whisper of the grave. “I am Death, and you”—she pointed a finger, so casual yet so damning—“you will kneel.”

The man’s defiance crumbled in an instant. His knees buckled beneath him as if pulled down by an invisible force, and he collapsed, gasping for air as the weight of Rio’s presence bore down on him. The other men watched, horror-stricken, as their companion writhed on the ground, clutching his throat as though something unseen was strangling him.

A ripple of fear spread through the mob, their bravado shattered. Agatha could see it now—the realization sinking in, the dawning terror that they were no longer in control. They were at the mercy of something far beyond their comprehension.

One by one, they began to drop their weapons, their faces pale as the blood drained from them, eyes darting between Rio and the fallen man at her feet.

Rio’s gaze never wavered, never softened. “I won’t ask again,” she said, her words like the final toll of a bell.

The mob fled, leaving Beley and Rio alone in the charged silence. The air crackled with the remnants of fear, and the echo of retreating footsteps faded into the background like a haunting melody.

“You’re Death?” Beley asked, her breath coming in short gasps, the weight of her realization pressing down on her like a heavy shroud. She stared at Rio, wide-eyed, as if trying to reconcile the woman before her with the legend that loomed large in her mind.

“The one and only,” Rio replied, her tone almost playful, yet imbued with a chilling certainty. There was a power in her words that made the air around them feel thick, as if reality itself trembled at her very presence.

As Rio spoke, her gaze shifted to Agatha, who was making her way toward them, the fierce determination in her stride a balm to the lingering tension. Agatha’s presence was a grounding force, a reminder that amidst the chaos, there was something worth fighting for.

Agatha met Rio’s eyes, and in that moment, an understanding passed between them—an acknowledgment of the darkness they both carried, and the strength that came from wielding it together. The fear that had gripped the mob was nothing compared to the ferocity they could unleash when united.

“Are you alright?” Agatha asked, her voice low, concern etching itself into her features as she reached Beley’s side. The tension in her body began to ease as she scanned her friend, searching for any signs of harm.

“I am now,” Beley replied, her voice steadier as she collected herself, though the remnants of fear still danced in her eyes. She glanced at Rio, and Agatha could see the mix of awe and respect forming in her expression. “Thank you. I didn’t know…”

“That you were standing in the presence of Death?” Rio interjected, a smirk tugging at her lips. “Most don’t. It tends to catch people off guard.”

Agatha chuckled lightly, relief washing over her at the banter between them, a soothing balm to the earlier chaos. “You always did have a flair for the dramatic,” she teased, her eyes sparkling as she turned to Rio.

“Drama? I prefer to think of it as style,” Rio retorted with a wink, a glimmer of mischief dancing in her eyes. The air around her shifted, the heavy tension that had clung to them dissolving into something lighter, almost playful. It was a side of Rio that Agatha cherished—the one that could transform darkness into a playful tease.

“Are you even allowed to kill them?” Agatha asked, arching an eyebrow, the curiosity laced with a hint of disbelief. It was a serious question, but the amusement in her tone hinted at the warmth she felt toward Rio's audacity.

“No, but they don’t know that,” Rio answered, her lips curling into a sly grin. There was an edge of exhilaration in her words, a thrill that came from wielding power, even if just for a moment. The uncertainty of the situation only heightened the adrenaline thrumming through Agatha's veins, making her feel alive in a way that was both intoxicating and dangerous.

Agatha couldn’t help but smile back, a playful challenge igniting in her gaze. “You’re playing a dangerous game, Death.”

“Dangerous games are my specialty,” Rio replied, her voice dripping with allure, each word like silk that wrapped around them, weaving an intoxicating spell. There was a fierceness in her that Agatha adored, a magnetism that drew her in deeper, fueling the connection they shared.

Beley stood slightly to the side, a bemused expression on her face as she took in the exchange between the two witches. “I think I’d prefer to keep my life out of your games, thank you very much,” she quipped, her voice light, though the shadows of their earlier encounter lingered in the back of her mind.

Agatha turned to Beley, the playful spark in her eyes fading slightly as she remembered the gravity of their situation. “We need to get out of here. The mob might regroup, and they won’t be so easy to scare off next time,” she said, the urgency creeping back into her tone.

“Let’s move,” Rio commanded, her grip firm as she took Agatha’s hand, a silent promise of support and strength. The connection between them pulsed like an electric current, grounding Agatha even as adrenaline surged through her veins.

Beley nodded, shaking off the remnants of shock as she fell into step behind them. Together, they moved with purpose, the quiet determination in their stride echoing the bond they shared—a trio forged in the crucible of chaos and magic.

As they navigated the narrow path out of the village, the early morning light began to filter through the trees, casting dappled shadows on the ground. Each step away from the scene of confrontation brought a sense of relief, yet the tension lingered in the air, thick with unspoken worries.

“So, where is your coven?” Agatha asked, breaking the silence, her tone laced with curiosity.

“Three days’ walk from here,” Beley replied, her voice steady, though a flicker of uncertainty danced in her eyes.

“Great.” Agatha rolled her eyes, playful frustration bubbling to the surface. “Three days? I suppose we’ll be getting our steps in, then.”

A soft chuckle escaped Beley, a momentary balm to the tension that clung to them. “Trust me, it’s worth it. They’ve got a strong protective circle. If you plan on facing Betha, you’re going to need them.”

Rio’s grip on Agatha’s hand tightened, a silent acknowledgment of their shared purpose. “We’ll get there,” she reassured, her voice calm yet resolute. “And when we do, we’ll ensure your coven is ready to stand with us.”

Agatha nodded, feeling the pulse of determination swell within her, a fierce rhythm that matched her heart. She wouldn’t admit it, but a part of her was intrigued by Betha—the dark allure of her power and the pull it exerted. There was something dangerously intoxicating about it, an invitation to dive deeper into the shadows.

As they pressed on, the trees around them began to thicken, the dappled light shifting into a mosaic of shadows and beams, creating a breathtaking yet eerie atmosphere. Each step deeper into the woods felt like stepping into another realm, one filled with whispers of magic and the weight of unspoken dangers lurking just out of sight.

“I hope they’re ready for a fight,” Agatha mused, glancing at Beley, whose expression had hardened into one of determination.

“They will be,” Beley assured, her voice gaining strength, as if fueled by the very essence of the forest around them. “My coven is not one to back down. We stand together, and we’ll protect what’s ours.”

“Stand together? Yet you left and betrayed Betha, leaving her to fend for her own,” Agatha accused, her words sharp, cutting through the air like a blade. The bitterness of betrayal lingered on her tongue, a painful reminder of her own past. She could still taste the acrid sting of her own coven’s disloyalty, a wound that hadn’t fully healed.

Rio felt the weight of Agatha’s accusation, her own heart tightening in sympathy. She squeezed Agatha’s hand, a silent gesture of solidarity, grounding her amidst the rising tension.

Beley’s expression shifted, the determination in her eyes momentarily wavering, revealing the flicker of guilt beneath her steadfast facade. “It’s not that simple,” she replied, her voice low, edged with regret. “Betha— she... she became something else. We didn’t leave her; we were forced to protect ourselves. You have to understand—”

“Understand what?” Agatha interjected, her voice rising, fueled by the memories of her own coven’s abandonment. “That self-preservation is more important than loyalty? That when the darkness comes, you run instead of fighting?”

Rio’s grip tightened, sensing the storm brewing within Agatha, but also recognizing the turmoil within Beley. “Agatha, wait,” she urged, her voice soft but firm.

“No, Rio. This is important,” Agatha insisted, her eyes locked on Beley, searching for the truth behind her words. “You can’t claim to protect your coven while leaving someone to the wolves. That’s not how it works.”

Beley stood her ground, her chin lifting slightly as she met Agatha’s fierce gaze. “I didn’t abandon her. I had to make a choice—a choice that kept me alive to fight another day. Choices like that aren’t easy, especially in this world.”

“Choices or excuses?” Agatha shot back, her voice rising, brimming with a mix of frustration and fury. “Now, when she’s too dangerous to handle, we are forced to step in, and what? What are we going to do with her? Kill her? Is that the plan?” She turned to Rio, seeking her lover's insight, the weight of her words hanging in the air between them.

Rio’s expression was contemplative, her eyes reflecting the tension that knotted in Agatha’s stomach. “It’s not about killing her,” she began, her voice steady, grounding the moment with a calm authority. “It’s about understanding the threat she poses and finding a way to neutralize it without resorting to death unless absolutely necessary.”

“Understanding?” Agatha scoffed, incredulous, her voice laced with disbelief. “She’s already crossed a line. Betha won’t stop until she’s consumed everything in her path. We are going to kill her, aren’t we?” The words hung heavy in the air, and Agatha felt a deep-seated unease coil within her, churning with the knowledge that, under different

circumstances, she could have easily been Betha. If it wasn’t for Death, if it wasn’t for Rio’s intervention, that darkness could have claimed her too.

Beley shifted, her gaze faltering under the weight of Agatha's accusation. “No one wants to kill her,” she insisted, though the tremor in her voice hinted at the uncertainty underlying her conviction. “We’re trying to save her from herself.”

“Save her?” Agatha snapped, frustration sparking like wildfire. “What makes you think she even wants saving? People like Betha thrive in chaos; they feed off destruction. You think we can just sit down and have a tea party and talk it out?”

Rio stepped in, her presence grounding. “We don’t know what drives Betha. She’s not just a monster; she’s a person with a story—a history that shaped her. Maybe we can find a way to reach her, to remind her of who she once was.”

“But what if that doesn’t work?” Agatha countered, her voice rising. “What if talking just makes her angrier? I’ve seen what that kind of anger can do. It’s a dark pit with no way out. You can’t reason with someone who’s lost everything.”

“Maybe so,” Rio acknowledged, her tone steady but softened with understanding. “But we can’t let fear dictate our actions either.”

The tension in the air crackled as Agatha searched Rio’s eyes, desperate for reassurance, for understanding. “You think I’m afraid?” she shot back, though her voice wavered slightly, betraying the vulnerability beneath her fierce exterior. “I’m not afraid of Betha.”

Rio grabbed Agatha and pulled her to the side, her hands resting on Agatha’s back, grounding her in a moment that felt like eternity. “You’re not her,” Rio replied, her voice a soothing balm, firm yet gentle, an anchor amidst the storm of Agatha’s emotions. “You’re stronger than you know. You’ve chosen a different path, and I’ll stand by you, no matter what.” Agatha felt the warmth of Rio’s touch seep into her skin, steadying her racing heart, the surge of uncertainty and fear slowly ebbing away. It was in these fleeting moments of connection that she found clarity. “But what if I can’t handle it? What if I lose myself in the chaos?”

Rio leaned in closer, her forehead resting against Agatha’s, a gesture so intimate it sent a shiver of warmth through them both. “Then you won’t be alone in the dark. I’ll be there to pull you back, to remind you who you are.” The conviction in Rio’s voice wrapped around Agatha like a protective cloak, shielding her from the shadows of doubt that loomed large.

“And who am I?” Agatha asked, her voice barely a whisper, laced with vulnerability and curiosity. She searched Rio’s gaze, longing for the answer to tether her to the ground beneath her unsteady feet.

“Mine,” Rio replied, her voice thick with a mix of possessiveness and affection. The word hung in the air like an incantation, binding them together in a way that felt both exhilarating and terrifying. It was a declaration, an undeniable truth that resonated deep within Agatha’s soul.

A flush crept across Agatha's cheeks, a warmth blooming at the center of her being. This is what it felt like—to belong, to be home. It was a sensation she had chased through the tangled labyrinth of her past, an elusive whisper that always seemed just out of reach. But now, here with Rio, it enveloped her like a soft, comforting blanket, wrapping around her heart and mind.

“This place is a mess,” Agatha remarked as they stepped into the tavern, her nose wrinkling at the overwhelming stench of stale ale and sweat. The dimly lit room was filled with rowdy patrons, their drunken laughter echoing off the walls like a chorus of chaos. Plates of half-eaten food littered the tables, and she could only imagine the state of the beds awaiting them in their room.

“I think... it’s cozy,” Beley said, though even her voice lacked conviction, a hint of discomfort seeping through her facade.

“Yeah, we already know how cozy you feel around humans,” Agatha quipped, rolling her eyes in playful exasperation. “Come on, Rio, there must be a town not far away from here. Please?”

“It’s getting dark,” Rio replied, her tone firm yet gentle, as she surveyed the rowdy scene behind them.

“And since when are we afraid of the dark?” Agatha shot back, her voice laced with defiance. The shadows held no fear for her; they were familiar, almost comforting in their embrace.

Rio’s gaze softened as she took in Agatha’s fiery spirit. “We’re not afraid, but you, my dear, must rest,” she said, her eyes locking onto Agatha’s leg. Agatha thought she hid her pain well, but apparently nothing escaped Rio’s keen observation.

The memory of their recent encounter with the fierce bandit flashed in Agatha’s mind, vivid and sharp. He had approached with a swagger, convinced he could take on the likes of them. But he hadn’t stood a chance against their combined power. Yet, in the chaos, he had managed to sneak up behind her, knocking Agatha to the ground, her ankle twisting painfully beneath her.

With a surge of adrenaline, she had retaliated, channeling her magic into a blast that sent him reeling, while Rio had effortlessly claimed his soul, guiding it to the afterlife with the grace of a master. Beley had stood there, wide-eyed and dumbfounded, a mere spectator to the fluid dance of power that unfolded between Agatha and Rio.

But yes, the pain in her ankle was undeniable, a throbbing reminder of her vulnerability. As much as she wanted to dismiss it, Agatha couldn’t ignore the way it flared with each movement, the ache a stark contrast to the exhilarating rush of their victory.

“I can’t believe I let him sneak up on me,” Agatha muttered, a mix of frustration and embarrassment coloring her tone. “I’m better than that.”

“You are better than that,” Rio assured her, her voice soothing, “But even the strongest can have moments of oversight. You fought back brilliantly, and that’s what matters.”

Agatha felt a warmth bloom in her chest at Rio’s words, yet the ache in her ankle persisted, reminding her of her limitations. She glanced down, seeing the slight swelling, a clear sign that she needed to heed Rio’s insistence on resting.

“Fine, I’ll take a moment,” Agatha conceded, her tone begrudging but tinged with the understanding that Rio was right. “But only if you promise to keep me entertained while I do.”

Rio’s lips curved into a playful smile, the mischief in her eyes returning. “Oh, I have a few tales that would make even the bravest of souls shiver. Shall I tell you about the time I met a witch who thought she could top me?”

Agatha blushed "if I remember correctly I think she did, twice"

Rio’s smile deepened, a glint of amusement dancing in her eyes. Leaning in closer, the warm scent of her presence enveloping Agatha like a spell. “You know I don’t lose easily. That witch may have gotten the better of me— twice—but I certainly left a mark.”

Agatha felt her cheeks heat, the familiar thrill of their banter igniting something vibrant within her. “You say that like it was all part of your grand plan,” she shot back, a playful smirk tugging at her lips. “Let’s not pretend you weren’t just a little caught off guard.”

Beley took a cautious step toward the tavern, shaking her head as she muttered, “Too much information,” under her breath. The disapproval laced with incredulity made Agatha chuckle even more.

“Aw, come on, Beley,” Agatha called out, amusement brightening her tone. “You can’t tell me you’re not at least a little curious about Rio’s legendary encounters.”

Beley rolled her eyes, but the corner of her mouth twitched upwards, betraying her resolve. “Curiosity killed the witch, remember?”

“Oh, but it also made for some excellent stories,” Rio interjected, her voice silky and playful. “And I assure you, my tales of intrigue are worth every ounce of curiosity. Just think of them as lessons in what not to do when dealing with powerful witches.”

Chapter 13

Chapter Summary

“She’s asleep,” Rio whispered, her tone guarded, the cadence of her words shifting slightly as if she were speaking to someone she didn’t entirely trust.

Agatha kept still, her breath shallow as she tried to discern the source of Rio’s conversation. The air in the room felt heavier now, as though it had been laced with a hidden tension that hadn't been there moments before.

Agatha’s suspicions were confirmed when she recognized the sharp, hushed tone of the other voice. Beley. She kept her eyes closed, feigning sleep, straining to hear more of the tense exchange.

“You can’t,” Beley’s voice was a mix of frustration and warning, her words clipped, almost desperate. “Their decision was final.”

Agatha’s pulse quickened as Beley’s words echoed in her mind—their decision was final. What decision? A chill ran down her spine as she lay there, pretending to sleep, her senses heightened, every muscle in her body coiled with tension.

Agatha stirred as soft murmurs pulled her from the edge of sleep, her body still warm from the bath, wrapped in the quiet comfort of their shared intimacy. Her eyes remained closed, but her senses were on high alert as she became aware of Rio’s voice, low and quiet, carrying a note of seriousness that instantly piqued her curiosity.

“She’s asleep,” Rio whispered, her tone guarded, the cadence of her words shifting slightly as if she were speaking to someone she didn’t entirely trust. Rio, ever unflappable, remained calm, though Agatha didn’t miss the subtle threat buried in her even tone. “Let me talk to her,” Rio had said, but it wasn’t a request. It was a statement, one laced with undeniable authority.

Beley hesitated, her frustration palpable in the silence that followed, but she didn’t push back again. Agatha heard her soft exhale of resignation.

Then came Rio’s final words, her voice lowering, almost a whisper now. “I’ll see you in the morning.”

The door clicked shut behind Beley, and the room fell into an eerie stillness, broken only by the faint sound of Agatha’s breathing.

Her heart pounded as she wondered what Beley meant—what decision? What were they keeping from her?

Seconds stretched into what felt like hours before she felt Rio’s presence near her again. A soft shift of the mattress, the subtle brush of Rio’s hand against her skin. Agatha kept her eyes closed, still feigning sleep.

Agatha’s breath hitched ever so slightly, betraying her. Rio’s touch was gentle, almost absentminded as her fingers weaved through the strands of Agatha’s hair, coaxing the truth from her without a single forceful word.

“I know you’re awake,” Rio murmured, her voice low, almost soothing. But there was an edge to it, something beneath the surface—a weight of knowledge or secrets not yet spoken.

Agatha didn’t open her eyes immediately, letting the silence stretch between them for a heartbeat longer, wondering how much Rio knew, how much she had overheard.

“What gave me away?” she finally whispered, the words barely audible as her eyelids fluttered open to meet Rio’s steady gaze.

Rio’s lips curled into a small, knowing smile, her thumb tracing the line of Agatha’s jaw. “You’re never this still when you’re asleep.”

Agatha swallowed, trying to push down the unease creeping up her spine. “What was that about? With Beley?”

Rio’s hand stilled for a fraction of a second before she resumed her gentle stroking, a calculated pause before speaking. “Something we’ll handle in the morning,” she said softly. “Right now, all you need to worry about is resting.”

But Agatha could feel the tension thrumming just beneath Rio’s calm surface, could sense the storm gathering on the horizon, one they were hurtling toward, whether they were ready or not.

The morning crept in slowly, a pale, gray light filtering through the narrow window, casting long shadows across the room. Agatha stirred, her body heavy with the remnants of sleep, the weight of the night still clinging to her limbs. The bed was warm, but beside her, Rio’s absence was like a void, the sheets cold where her body had been.

Agatha stretched, feeling the pull of her muscles as she rolled onto her back, eyes blinking up at the ceiling. The events of last night were a blur— Beley’s sharp words, Rio’s quiet, controlled anger, the tension that had hummed in the air long after the door had closed behind their visitor. She sat up slowly, her mind foggy, the echoes of their whispered conversation swirling in her head. Whatever it was, it wasn’t over. Not yet.

Pulling herself out of bed, Agatha padded across the room, her bare feet cold against the wooden floor. She spotted Rio standing by the small window, her silhouette sharp against the soft, gray light of dawn. She was dressed already, her cloak draped loosely around her shoulders, her gaze fixed on something outside—though Agatha had the distinct impression that Rio wasn’t truly looking at anything at all.

For a moment, Agatha just watched her, taking in the way Rio’s posture seemed almost tense, as if she were holding something back, bracing herself for the inevitable.

“Morning,” Agatha said softly, her voice cutting through the stillness of the room.

Rio didn’t turn around right away, but Agatha saw the subtle shift in her shoulders, a slight exhale as if she had been waiting for that sound. “Morning,” Rio finally replied, her voice low, almost distant.

Agatha crossed the room, the quiet between them thick, almost oppressive. She came to stand beside Rio, following her gaze out the window. The world outside was quiet, still wrapped in the cool embrace of dawn, but Agatha could feel the tension simmering beneath the surface, waiting to spill over.

“Are you going to tell me what’s going on?” Agatha asked, her voice steady, though she felt the weight of the question in her chest.

Rio’s jaw tightened ever so slightly, but she didn’t answer right away. Instead, she let the silence stretch on, the tension building between them like an unspoken promise. Agatha watched her, the weight of the moment pressing down on her, waiting for the words she could feel Rio carefully measuring, calculating, deciding how much to reveal and how much to withhold.

When Rio finally spoke, her voice was low, deliberate, cutting through the quiet like a blade. “Does the name Lilia Calderu tell you something?”

Agatha frowned, the name unfamiliar on her tongue. “No. Why?”

Rio’s gaze sharpened, her eyes narrowing as if she was assessing something, some unseen thread connecting the pieces of a puzzle Agatha couldn’t yet see. “For some reason, she doesn’t want you near her coven,” Rio said, her tone edged with something dark, something foreboding. “Beley’s coven.”

The mention of Beley made Agatha’s chest tighten, her mind flashing back to the tension last night, the way Beley’s voice had been laced with warning, with desperation. Agatha’s stomach twisted. “What does this have to do with me?”

Rio exhaled slowly, her gaze drifting away from Agatha, out toward the horizon again, as if she could find the answers out there in the dawn’s pale light. “I don’t know yet,” she admitted, her voice quieter now, almost reluctant. “But Lilia Calderu is powerful. Old magic. And whatever her reasons, she sees you as a threat.”

Agatha’s pulse quickened, but she couldn’t stop the frustrated edge creeping into her voice. “A threat? To what? I’ve never even heard of her, much less her coven.”

Rio’s eyes flickered with something unreadable, a shadow passing over her expression. “That’s the part I don’t understand,” she murmured, her voice distant, as if she were speaking more to herself than to Agatha. “But I intend to find out.”

A knot of unease tightened in Agatha’s chest. The weight of this new information pressed down on her, the questions it raised swirling in her mind, unanswered, unsettling. The idea of being caught in the middle of something she didn’t understand, something beyond her control—it was enough to make her skin prickle with dread.

But even as the fear gnawed at her, there was something else, something darker, deeper. A flicker of defiance sparked in her chest, and she straightened, her jaw setting with determination. “Then we’ll find out together,” she said, her voice firm, unyielding. “I won’t be pushed around by someone I’ve never met. If Lilia Calderu has a problem with me, I’ll face her myself.”

Rio turned to her, her gaze locking onto Agatha’s with a sharp intensity, the weight of her words hanging in the air between them. A slow, almost imperceptible smile tugged at the corner of her lips, a mixture of admiration and caution. “That’s what I thought you’d say,” she murmured, her voice low, threaded with a kind of reluctant amusement. But there was a warning in her eyes, dark and unyielding. “But Agatha, if we weren’t welcome before, we are definitely not welcome now.”

Agatha felt a cold chill slide down her spine, not from fear, but from the realization of how far things had spiraled beyond her control. She sat up straighter, the morning light casting long shadows around them, bathing everything in a strange, muted glow. “What do you mean?”

Rio’s expression hardened, the playful edge gone, replaced by something more serious, more dangerous. “Last night… Beley wasn’t just frustrated. She was scared. Whatever’s happening with her coven, Lilia Calderu’s made it clear we’re not to interfere. She’s drawn the line, and crossing it will come at a price.”

A beat of silence passed between them, the weight of Rio’s words sinking in. Agatha clenched her fists, the familiar burn of defiance igniting in her chest. “When has that ever stopped me?” she said, her voice edged with determination, her lips curling into a bitter smile.

Rio chuckled softly, though there was no real humor in it. “You don’t understand, Agatha. This isn’t like the trials you’ve faced before. Calderu’s magic is… ancient. Old and twisted in ways even you can’t predict. She’s woven herself into the roots of that coven, and if we challenge her—if we push any further—there will be consequences.”

Agatha met Rio’s gaze, the weight of Rio's words sinking in, but she refused to let it break her resolve. “Let her try. I won’t be intimidated by some hidden witch playing puppet master.”

Rio sighed softly, her eyes flickering with a mix of understanding and concern. “I know,” she said, her voice quieter now. “But we do need her on our side if we plan to find Betha.”

The mention of Betha sent a sharp sting through Agatha’s chest, and before she could stop herself, the hurt welled up, unbidden and raw. “So… you don’t want me there?” The words tumbled out, softer than she intended, the vulnerability in her voice cutting through the bravado.

Rio’s eyes widened in surprise, and in an instant, she was moving toward Agatha, closing the distance between them as though the mere space between them was too much. “What? No—no, never,” Rio said, her voice almost a plea, laced with urgency. The air between them thickened, heavy with emotions neither of them could fully name. Rio exhaled slowly, her hand falling from Agatha’s face but not moving far, her fingers lingering at the edge of Agatha’s sleeve, as if still unwilling to break their connection completely. The name, Lilia Calderu, hung in the air between them, heavy with implications neither of them had fully unraveled yet. Agatha’s brows knit together, her mind racing through possibilities, but none of them made sense—not yet. “Beley wouldn’t tell you?”

Rio’s lips twisted into a bitter smile, her fingers tracing absent patterns against Agatha’s arm, a restless energy simmering just beneath her skin. “Beley wouldn’t tell me much of anything. She barely trusts me, let alone you.”

Agatha’s lips curved into a mirthless smile of her own. “I can’t imagine why.”

The faintest chuckle escaped Rio, but it was humorless, a dark sound that echoed between them like the acknowledgment of some grim, unspoken truth. “You know as well as I do, Agatha. Witches like Calderu—witches with something to hide—don’t take kindly to anyone who might dig too deep.”

Agatha nodded slowly, her mind still turning over the name like a puzzle she couldn’t quite solve. “So we dig.”

Rio’s smile returned, sharper this time, more dangerous, like the edge of a blade hidden in velvet. “So we dig.”

And just like that, the air between them shifted, the brief vulnerability giving way to the steely determination that had always driven them forward, no matter the odds. Together, they would find the truth, no matter how deep they had to go or how many enemies they would make along the way.

Beley made her choice - her resolve hardening like tempered steel.There was no room for hesitation, no time to wait for Agatha and Rio to catch up. She would meet with her coven, her heart set on easing their fears, perhaps even warming them to the idea of Agatha’s arrival. It was a delicate balance she had to tread, each step potentially stirring old resentments.

Meanwhile, in the quiet aftermath of their conversation, Rio felt the familiar flicker of frustration mixed with urgency. She needed answers about Lilia. And she needed them fast.Without hesitation, she reached for a pieace of paper, a fleeting pulse of magic guiding her fingers as she crafted a message to Aline. Despite their tumultuous past, Aline had connections, whispers that traveled through the intricate web of witching society. Perhaps she would know something—anything—that could provide clarity and help them navigate this precarious situation.

Each encounter, every stolen moment of magic replayed in her mind, whispering secrets she had buried deep within. Rio’s fingers stilled for just a heartbeat, a brief pause as if she was waiting, sensing the turmoil beneath Agatha’s skin. Then, with the same delicate care, her touch resumed, threading through Agatha’s hair like a lifeline. “I’m here,” Rio whispered, her voice low and steady, an anchor in the quiet darkness. She didn’t push, didn’t demand, just offered her presence, inviting Agatha to unravel if she was ready to share.

Agatha’s voice trembled as she forced the words out, the weight of her realization crashing over her like a tidal wave. “She never wanted me,” Agatha whispered, her voice breaking under the strain. “My mother… it makes sense now. All of it.”

Agatha stirred, her eyelids fluttering open to find the room still cloaked in darkness. The weight of Rio’s fingers threading through her hair was a familiar comfort, grounding her in the quiet. She turned her head slightly, eyes meeting Rio’s.

“Hey,” Rio whispered, her voice soft, intimate, as if trying not to disturb the night itself. She leaned down, pressing a kiss to Agatha’s forehead, her lips warm against cool skin. “It’s late, you should sleep.”

Agatha exhaled, but the knot of tension in her chest refused to loosen. The warmth of Rio’s touch, usually so soothing, barely cut through the haze of unease swirling inside her. “I can’t,” she whispered, her voice cracking at the edges, as if the weight of everything still pressed too heavily for rest.

Rio’s fingers stilled for just a heartbeat, a brief pause as if she was waiting, sensing the turmoil beneath Agatha’s skin. Then, with the same delicate care, her touch resumed, threading through Agatha’s hair like a lifeline. “I’m here,” Rio whispered, her voice low and steady, an anchor in the quiet darkness. She didn’t push, didn’t demand, just offered her presence, inviting Agatha to unravel if she was ready to share.

Agatha’s voice trembled as she forced the words out, the weight of her realization crashing over her like a tidal wave. “She never wanted me,” Agatha whispered, her voice breaking under the strain. “My mother… it makes sense now. All of it.”

Rio didn’t speak, didn’t try to fill the silence with meaningless reassurances. Instead, her fingers kept their steady rhythm, curling through Agatha’s hair, offering comfort in the only way she knew how. Each touch was gentle, coaxing her back from the edge of the abyss.

Agatha’s voice trembled, her words spilling out in uneven breaths. “She was probably forced to marry my father,” she whispered, each syllable heavy with old wounds. “They never got along, you know.”

Rio’s touch remained constant, a lifeline in the sea of Agatha’s unraveling memories.

“He left when I was three,” Agatha continued, her voice quieter now, as if speaking the truth aloud would make the past too real, too raw. The weight of abandonment settled over her, heavy as stone, as if she had been carrying it all this time, waiting for the moment it would crush her. And now, here it was.

The silence between them thickened, filled only by the sound of Agatha’s unsteady breaths. Rio didn’t say anything—there was nothing she could say to undo years of neglect and loss. Instead, she wrapped her arms more tightly around Agatha, a wordless promise that she wasn’t alone anymore, no matter how deep the scars went.

“And now I find out she had someone else all along,” Agatha’s voice cracked, bitterness seeping into her words. “Lilia. All those years, and she never told me. She chose her. She always chose someone else over me.” The confession hung in the air like a wound laid bare, raw and bleeding. Agatha clenched the sheets beneath her, her knuckles white with the force of it.

“I was never enough.” She choked out the words, her tears staining Rio’s shirt as her body shook with a grief she hadn’t allowed herself to feel until now.

Rio kissed the top of her head, her own heart breaking for the woman she held in her arms. “You are enough,” she whispered fiercely, her fingers still tangling through Agatha’s hair, trying to soothe the storm raging within her. “You always have been. And now you’re mine. Only mine.”

Agatha’s sobs began to quiet, though the pain still curled deep in her chest, raw and unrelenting, an ache that no words could soothe. The emptiness left by her mother lingered, vast and dark, as if nothing could ever fill it.

Through the tears, her voice trembled, barely holding together. “What are we going to do?” she asked, broken and small between the sharp edges of her breaths.

Rio's fingers never stopped tracing along Agatha’s skin, a steady anchor in the storm of emotions. “We’re going to find Lilia,” she said, her voice low and steady, carrying a weight of promise. “And we’re going to make her talk.”

Agatha flinched at the thought—Lilia, the ghost of her past, the woman who had taken what little was left of her mother’s heart. The idea of facing her, demanding answers, stirred something both fragile and fierce within her.

“Force her?” Agatha’s words barely escaped her lips, her mind reeling at the possibilities.

Rio’s hand paused, her grip firming ever so slightly. “Yes,” she said, no hesitation in her voice. “If she won’t cooperate willingly, then we’ll make her.”

There was no gentleness in Rio now—only cold, unyielding certainty. The promise of power, of control, of finally wrenching the truth from the shadows. Agatha felt the pull of it, the danger wrapped in Rio’s unwavering resolve.

For a moment, it frightened her—the thought of what they might do, how far they might go. But the fear was eclipsed by something stronger, something buried beneath the hurt and the tears. A need for the truth. A need for closure.

“And we’ll do it together,” Rio added, her voice softening only for Agatha. Her lips brushed Agatha’s hair, grounding her in the dark. “No more running. No more hiding.”

Agatha’s breath caught, her chest tightening, but beneath the weight of the night, something had shifted. It wasn’t peace, no—peace was still far away, hidden behind too many layers of pain and unanswered questions. But it was something else, a kind of determination, a small flicker of resolve. A step toward the truth she had always been too afraid to face but could no longer run from.

“And Aline? Edwin?” Agatha asked, her voice still fragile, cracking under the strain of everything that had been revealed. “What about the prophecy?”

Rio’s fingers stilled in her hair, the warmth of her touch cooling as a dark glint flickered in her eyes. Something dangerous, primal, flickered there—a shadow Agatha had glimpsed before but never quite this clearly.

“They can all burn,” Rio said, her voice soft but lethal, each word soaked in a quiet, unrelenting fury. “Aline, Edwin, the prophecy—it doesn’t matter. They don’t matter.”

Agatha felt a chill run through her at the cold finality of Rio’s words. It wasn’t the first time she had seen this side of her, but it still sent a ripple of uncertainty through her. Rio had always carried darkness with her—after all, she was Death incarnate—but now, it felt different, more personal, more dangerous.

Agatha’s heart thundered in her chest, a wild storm of fear and desire warring inside her as she gazed at Rio. This was the woman who would raze the world to ash for her, the one who had walked through the very realm of death and would drag anyone who dared stand in their path into ruin. And yet, despite the danger that clung to Rio like a second skin, despite the darkness that swirled around her, Agatha couldn’t turn away.

No—wouldn’t turn away.

It wasn’t just Rio’s darkness that pulled her in like a tide she couldn’t resist. It was her own, the shadowed part of her soul that had always longed for something deeper, something darker. It called out to Rio’s fire, not in fear, but in yearning. Craving. The darkness in her didn’t shrink back—it leaned in, wanting more, begging for it.

Rio’s eyes held her captive, burning with an intensity that both threatened to consume her and promised to keep her safe within that flame. Agatha could feel it—her own hunger rising to meet it, the edges of her restraint fraying as that dangerous connection between them deepened. It wasn’t love in its purest form, nor desire alone—it was something raw, something ancient. It was the pull of two forces meant to collide, to break and rebuild in each other’s embrace.

And in that moment, Agatha surrendered to it. Not in fear, not in hesitation —but in need. A need as dark and infinite as the abyss they both stood on the edge of.

She wanted more.

Chapter End Notes

We are getting closer to the end, so the chapters are shorter because I want to delay the inevitable.

but I am writing a sequel - let me know if you wish me to post it. for now, I want to thank all of you who left comments and kudos on this story, you are amazing.

Chapter 18

Chapter Summary

“Are you suicidal?” Rio’s voice cut through the moment, low and dangerous, each word sharp as a blade.

Agatha’s head snapped toward her, and there Rio stood, her dark hair tousled, eyes gleaming with quiet menace. She leaned against the doorframe, her presence electric, filling the room with the kind of tension that made the air itself feel too thick to breathe.

Edwin’s hand fell away from Agatha’s face as if it had burned him. His gaze flicked to Rio, his confidence crumbling beneath her stare.

Rio didn’t move, but her presence was a warning, a predator’s gaze fixed on prey. “Touch her again,” she said, her voice a slow, deliberate threat, “and the next thing you’ll see is the inside of your eyelids.”

Agatha slipped quietly from the bed, intending to fetch some water, but as she padded toward the kitchen, she caught sight of Edwin sitting at the table, a cup of tea cradled between his hands, steam curling lazily upward in the dim light.

“Can’t sleep?” she asked, her voice low as she moved past him toward the sink.

He looked up, a tired smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. “Lilith snores,” he muttered, rubbing the back of his neck, as though the weight of the night had settled there, dragging him down. Silence stretched between them, thick and awkward, filling the small space like smoke.

“And you?” he asked eventually, his voice soft but edged with curiosity.

Agatha paused mid-step, her fingers curling around the glass, the cool press of it grounding her. She let out a breath she didn’t know she was holding, her voice dry with a bitterness she hadn’t quite shaken. “Well, I just found out my mother had a lover,” she said, lifting the glass to her lips and taking a slow sip, as if water alone could wash down the truth.

“I really thought you knew,” he said, guilt threading through his voice like a frayed seam.

“Well, I didn’t.” Her words were sharp, a blade drawn to keep emotions at bay. She turned on her heel, ready to slip back into the guest room, to Rio, where the chaos of the night might unravel into something bearable.

But then, softly—“Agatha.”

The sound of her name stopped her, and she turned to find him standing there, not as the man he’d become but as the boy she had once trusted, the one who had stood beside her when the world made a little more sense.

“Please,” he whispered, the word raw and unsteady as he rose from his chair, drifting toward her like a ghost from a past they had both long buried.

Agatha stood frozen, the space between them tightening with unsaid things —old wounds, fractured promises, the ache of things left unresolved. Edwin stopped just short of touching her, his hand hovering in the space between them, uncertain, as if crossing that final inch might shatter whatever fragile thread still tethered them.

“Please,” he repeated, quieter this time, the word laced with something that felt too close to regret. His eyes searched hers, as if hoping to find in them the girl she used to be, the one who might still believe in him.

Agatha felt a flicker of something—nostalgia, maybe, or the ghost of trust long abandoned—but it was drowned beneath the weight of everything that had happened. The girl he remembered was gone, buried under years of betrayal and magic and loss.

She exhaled slowly, the breath shaky, as she shook her head. “I can’t,” she whispered, the words heavier than she intended. Her voice cracked, and in that crack, something unspoken passed between them—a shared grief for what might have been.

“You’ve changed,” Edwin murmured, his eyes soft with something dangerously close to hope. “But the real you… she’s still in there. I see her, Agatha. I see you.” His hand rose slowly, almost hesitantly, and when his palm cupped her cheek, it was like touching a memory—something fleeting, too delicate to hold.

Agatha’s heart stumbled in her chest, torn between the warmth of his words and the cold truth she carried with her now. Before she could respond, a shadow shifted in the doorway.

“Are you suicidal?” Rio’s voice cut through the moment, low and dangerous, each word sharp as a blade.

Agatha’s head snapped toward her, and there Rio stood, her dark hair tousled, eyes gleaming with quiet menace. She leaned against the doorframe, her presence electric, filling the room with the kind of tension that made the air itself feel too thick to breathe.

Edwin’s hand fell away from Agatha’s face as if it had burned him. His gaze flicked to Rio, his confidence crumbling beneath her stare.

Rio didn’t move, but her presence was a warning, a predator’s gaze fixed on prey. “Touch her again,” she said, her voice a slow, deliberate threat, “and the next thing you’ll see is the inside of your eyelids.”

Agatha didn’t flinch. Instead, she felt that familiar, unsettling pull—her darkness curling toward Rio’s like smoke to a flame.

The room seemed to shrink around them, the silence thick and suffocating. Agatha could feel Edwin retreating—not physically, not yet, but in the way people do when they realize they’re standing at the edge of something far more dangerous than they imagined. His hands hung awkwardly at his sides, the boyish hope that had flickered in his eyes extinguished, leaving only guilt and confusion in its place.

“Come with me, Agatha,” he said quietly His voice was soft, almost fragile, like a thread that might snap if pulled too tightly. Agatha felt it—the weight of old promises, the memory of safer days, lingering between them like a half-forgotten song. For a moment, it was tempting, dangerously so, that flicker of what could have been. But temptation was a fleeting thing, and it paled in comparison to the gravity pulling her toward Rio.

“You know I can’t,” Agatha whispered, though the words sounded like an apology, a wound she couldn’t stop from reopening. Edwin’s face twisted— not with anger, but with a sadness so profound it threatened to swallow them both.

“I could keep you safe,” he said, his voice cracking under the weight of everything left unsaid. “We could leave all this—start over. No magic, no prophecies... Just us.”

Agatha wanted to laugh at the simplicity of it, but all she could manage was a bitter smile. There is no ‘just us’anymore, she thought. There hadn’t been for a long time. Maybe never.

Behind her, Rio’s presence burned like a fire too wild to contain, casting shadows that danced along the walls and swallowed the room whole. She didn’t need to say a word; her silence was heavier than any threat, a promise lingering just beneath the surface, waiting to break free.

Agatha felt it—the possessiveness, the simmering rage barely masked by her calm exterior. And yet, beneath all of that, there was something else: the unwavering certainty that Rio was hers, that she belonged nowhere else but by her side.

“I can’t leave her, Edwin,” Agatha whispered, her voice low and steady, though each word carved a deeper wound into whatever fragile thing they had left between them.

Edwin looked at her as if she had struck him, the color draining from his face. “She’s dangerous,” he whispered, desperation creeping into his tone. “Agatha, she’ll ruin you—she’ll burn you down until there’s nothing left.”

Agatha turned toward him, her gaze steady, but there was no anger in her expression—only sadness. “Maybe,” she admitted. “But if I burn, I’ll burn with her.”

Rio’s hand rested firmly against the small of Agatha’s back, the touch searing through the fabric like a brand, possessive without needing to be. Agatha leaned into it, instinctively grounding herself in the quiet strength Rio offered. There was a finality in the gesture, a silent but undeniable line drawn between past and present.

Agatha’s gaze flicked back to Edwin, her expression unreadable, though the weight of her words was unmistakable. “Goodnight, Edwin,” she said softly, but there was no invitation left in her voice—only closure.

Without another word, Rio turned, her hand guiding Agatha as they moved in unison, a seamless rhythm forged in trust and defiance. Agatha didn’t look back, didn’t falter, as Rio led her down the dim hallway toward the guest room, the door clicking shut behind them like the closing of a chapter.

Inside the room, the night wrapped around them like a shroud, heavy and thick with things unspoken. Rio didn’t release her hold on Agatha, her hand still resting on her back as if anchoring her to the present, keeping her from slipping into the undertow of everything that had just unfolded. Agatha let out a slow, shuddering breath, her body sagging slightly against Rio’s.

“You okay?” Rio whispered, brushing her thumb over Agatha’s spine in slow, deliberate circles, grounding her with touch alone.

Agatha didn’t answer right away. She could feel the weight of Edwin’s words lingering on her skin, the ghost of what he had offered—something simpler, something safer. But safety was an illusion she had long since abandoned, and Rio... Rio was something else entirely. Dangerous, yes. But she was real.

“No,” Agatha finally whispered, burying her face against Rio’s shoulder. “But I will be.”

Rio’s arms tightened around her in response, holding her with a kind of fierceness that made it clear she wasn’t going anywhere, not tonight, not ever. “You don’t have to be okay,” she murmured against Agatha’s hair. “Not with me.”

Agatha let herself sink deeper into the embrace, the heat between them no longer just a fire but a promise: no matter how dark the path ahead, they would walk it together.

"Come to bed," Rio said softly, her breath warm against Agatha’s temple.

Agatha nodded, wordless, as Rio guided her toward the mattress. There was no urgency in their movements, just the quiet comfort of knowing the other was there. Rio settled in first, pulling Agatha into the circle of her arms like she was something precious, something worth guarding.

Agatha curled into her, fitting herself into the spaces only Rio could fill, her hand resting against Rio’s chest, feeling the steady rhythm of her heartbeat beneath her palm. The sound was grounding, a slow and deliberate reminder that, for now, they were still here, still together.

As they lay entwined, the darkness around them deepened, but within their cocoon of warmth, it felt almost like a sanctuary. Agatha's thoughts began to drift, swirling like shadows at the edge of her mind. She could still hear Edwin’s words echoing softly, the ache of his longing juxtaposed against the fierce love that burned in Rio’s embrace.

“Rio?” Agatha murmured, breaking the fragile silence that enveloped them.

“Hmm?” Rio replied, her voice a low, soothing hum, laced with sleep yet still sharp enough to hold Agatha’s attention.

“What if this prophecy really is true?” Agatha asked, her voice barely above a whisper, laden with the weight of her fears. “What if I’m destined to become something terrible?”

Rio shifted slightly, her fingers trailing along Agatha’s arm, drawing invisible patterns on her skin. “You can never be terrible,” Rio promised, her tone unwavering as if her words were a spell meant to ward off the shadows lurking at the edges of Agatha’s thoughts. “Even if the world sees you that way, I will always see you—you.”

“But what if I can’t control it?” Agatha pressed, her heart racing beneath the delicate caress of Rio’s touch. “What if I hurt someone? What if I hurt you?”

Rio's gaze locked onto Agatha's, fierce and unyielding, a flame that refused to be extinguished. “If you feel the darkness creeping in, if you ever feel like you might lose yourself, you come to me. We will face it together. You are mine, Agatha, and I’d be damned if I let anyone take you away from me —even the darkness itself.”

Agatha felt the weight of those words settle in her chest, an anchor in the tumultuous sea of her emotions. She didn’t know how to explain to Rio that the darkness had already claimed a part of her, weaving its way into her soul like an insidious vine. Instead, she closed the distance between them, kissing Rio softly, gently, allowing the warmth of her presence to envelop her. The scent of Rio wrapped around her, grounding her in that moment.

As their lips met, a soft spark ignited, sending a shiver down Agatha’s spine. It was a kiss filled with unspoken promises, an invitation to share their fears and desires in a world that felt increasingly dark. When she finally pulled back, breathless, Agatha looked into Rio’s eyes, the depths of which mirrored her own chaos, yet shone with unwavering resolve.

“I love you,” she whispered against Rio’s lips, the confession slipping free like a fragile secret released into the night.

Rio leaned in again, capturing Agatha’s mouth with fervor, the kiss igniting like wildfire between them. Passion poured from her, a torrential flood of emotion that wrapped around Agatha, enveloping her in warmth and desire. It was a kiss that spoke of promises unspoken, of fears faced together, of a love that defied the shadows lurking just beyond their reach.

“I love you too, Agatha Harkness,” Rio finally breathed, her voice thick with sincerity, her gaze a tempest of emotion. The weight of her words hung in the air, an unbreakable bond woven through the chaos of their lives.
